Below 560 px of page width they are icon-only with their names kept, except Allow 10 more turns, which keeps its words and moves the buttons to their own row so the title keeps the full width. The second row holds `Back to the project`, the state chip (the dot and the state word) and, with a pull request, a `#n` chip linking to GitHub with its badges; the state reason and the placement line follow. The brief's `Placed without Jev` chip waits for Jev placement with Why, because every phase 3 placement is the fallback (both built in phase 4, decision 483).
457. (D233) One note sits above the thread transcript when needed: nothing yet, a transcript that cannot be read right now (reports shown on their own), or only the most recent part shown. Report cards read `Report · turn {n}` (with `Recorded by Jevellan` when synthesized) with a status chip and a matching left rule (Progress muted, Done ok, Needs a decision magenta, Blocked warn), then the summary as Markdown, the question with its options as a read-only list (answers go through Waiting for you) and the tests. Changed files are not shown, because the brief lists only the status, summary, question, options and tests.
458. (D234) Settings, Git has the GitHub token card (decision 273) between GitHub connection and Check a project. It shows `Saved · updated {date}` or `Not set` without the last four characters, `Add token` until a token is saved and `Replace` afterwards, and `Remove` only with a saved token. Add and Replace open a modal with a password field that is focused on open and cleared whatever happens, saved through the settings save path (request id, waits for the hub); Remove is a danger confirmation. The token never reaches the page.
459. (D235) `scripts/test-server.mjs --projects` seeds the Projects browser fixture; without the flag the server behaves exactly as before. It starts the fake GitHub before the hub and saves three projects (`Projects fixture` with main policy and a GitHub-shaped remote redirected by `insteadOf` (decision 254), `Projects external`, which leaves git to the owner, and `Projects offline`, whose coordinator lives on the offline fixture device), two enabled accounts that are checked and discovered so a Claude and a Codex model can run, a GitHub token, a notebook written by the coordinator and a concluded thread index owned by the member (merged pull request #7), whose row answers `Remote threads arrive in phase 5.` until remote threads exist. Placement answers from the fake Jev wait for Jev placement (added in phase 4, decision 484). Since phase 5 that row opens `This thread was not found.`, because the fixture writes only its index row.
460. (D236) Scripted Projects turns: each adapter's turn script is matched by owner id and turn number, so concurrent coordinator and thread turns never take each other's scripts, and coordinator markers are matched only after the last event block header, because a fresh session's recap repeats earlier owner messages. Bridge calls are reported as tool events, as runtimes report MCP calls. `PJ3: add a greeting` starts the thread `Add a greeting` and replies `Started "Add a greeting".` (its 1 s pause was later replaced by a hold, decision 466); `PJ3 decision` asks the owner which greeting to use, with two options; other batches reply `Noted.`. Thread turns create and commit `greeting.txt` and report done with passing tests, or, for a `PJ3 long` thread, write tool rows to the native transcript every 350 ms and hold until released (a progress report) or aborted (no report).
461. (D237) A new pull request on the browser fixture starts with checks pending (`Checks running`), as CI queues runs on GitHub; the fake GitHub's own default stays `none` because the integration tests assert it.
462. (D238) Projects control routes share the fixture server's existing control listener (`port + 200`) and answer only with `--projects`. All are POST with versioned fixture documents: change a pull request's checks or mergeability, merge or close it; pulse both devices' project work; release a held scripted step by its marker; write the notebook as the coordinator; remove or restore the fixture token. Invalid documents answer 400 with the zod messages and unknown routes 404. `GET /projects/ready` answers 204 once every seed is in place and is the readiness check of the `--projects` web servers, whose `/api/health` answers before seeding ends.
463. (D239) Playwright runs `projects.spec.ts` in four `{layout}-projects` projects whose web servers add `--projects` and wait for `/projects/ready`; the base layouts ignore that spec. The vision expectation name pattern becomes `^(?:phase\d+|P?J\d+[a-z]?)-`, and all 252 tracked vision expectations keep their text.
464. (D240) Every Playwright web server stops with SIGTERM and a 15 s grace period before SIGKILL: the default SIGKILL never ran the fixture server's close and left a temporary root of about 25 MB per server per run.
465. (D241) `tests/e2e/projects.spec.ts` is one serial file with a 600 s per-test timeout, because its journeys build on one fresh `--projects` server in order. Brief copy is asserted as literals; placement lines, the tool one-liner and the start toast are built from the API views and compared exactly. Every screenshot is `PJ3-<step>-<layout>.png`, taken by one helper that dismisses toasts after the journey has read them, parks the pointer and asserts that nothing scrolls sideways, and every test asserts no page error. Against the planned outline, the waiting badge is asserted in the decision journey and `1 running` in the thread journey (a fresh server has neither), and phone-only screenshots record the sidebar drawer and the Waiting tab. Two test-only helpers: `tap` clicks sticky controls at their on-screen point (Playwright's click scrolls a stuck element's place in the document into view, which jumped the page), and `read` resends a GET once on `ECONNRESET` (a kept-alive connection the daemon closes after 5 s idle).
466. (D242) The scripted greeting coordinator turn holds after its first words until the release control route (or Stop), so `Coordinator is working…`, the chip and Stop stay on screen as long as the journey needs on a loaded four-layout run; the journey checks that the working line is still shown after its screenshot, then releases the turn. This replaces the 1 s pause of decision 460.
467. (D243) Product fixes found by the PJ3 evidence: Running rows tick every second while one is under a minute old (they read seconds while the clock ticked every 30 s); the notebook editor is focused without scrolling the page; from 1181 px the open inspector beside a Projects page is fixed at the right, because Chrome scrolled the page on every keystroke in a text field of the sticky column (conversation pages keep the sticky column); the sidebar list cap is a whole number of 34 px rows and the list snaps to rows; the thread page placement line clamps at three lines, so the device stays readable on phones.
468. (D244) The builder's visual review of the PJ3 screenshots, standing in for the vision checks that did not run, led to these fixes: a thread row's screen-reader state no longer escapes its title's clip (a long title made the phone page scroll sideways); the composer of both Projects pages fades only within its top padding, so the live bar and `Jump to latest` no longer sit over chat text (conversation pages keep the shared style); the sidebar scrolls the current project's row into its list once per project opened; report and decision cards align with their neighbours, and the Answered summary has a rounded focus ring; the spec parks the pointer before each capture, and its long-running thread has a title longer than its row, which guards the overflow fix on desktop and phone.
469. (D245) Thread transcripts leave out a completed `jevellan_thread_report` call whose summary is a recorded (not synthesized) report, because its card follows the turn and the raw tool row read as internal tooling. Running and failed calls stay, and so does a completed call that no recorded report carries, because a refused call can read as completed (decision 255).
470. (D246) `GET /api/project-work`, which every open page polls every 5 s (decision 438), reads only what the sidebar rows show: per project the thread indexes, the questions and the coordinator state. It no longer reads the work settings, the configuration or the accounts, each a hub request on a member. The project page's chip uses the same coordinator-state helper, and the work view is unchanged. Measured on the browser fixture (13 projects, 20 requests): on the member 2174 ms wall and 369 ms CPU per request before, 1131 ms and 186 ms after. Found by the phase 3 gate: J11 alone took 78 to 86 s against 71 to 78 s at the previous commit.
471. (D247) Read-only project hub operations (the settings, coordinator, coordinator status, thread, decision and notebook reads) are admitted outside the lifecycle gate, like GET requests; writes enter the gate once the operation is parsed, so an installer holding maintenance still refuses them with 503. Every operation is classified in a typed read and write table, so an operation added later cannot skip the gate unnoticed. Cause: every gated request writes `locks/activity.json` twice with a synchronous fsync, which blocked the hub's event loop for about 0.8 s of each 1.1 s member list request. With this change and decision 470 the member list takes 291 ms wall and 54.5 ms CPU, and J11 alone 73.8 to 75.6 s (previous commit 71.4 to 78.6 s); the regression test is in `tests/project-hub-store.test.ts`. Other member reads sent as POST keep the gate (pre-existing).
472. (D248) Phase 3 copies the decisions it implements as 428 to 472 in the format of decision 392: the interface decisions deferred until now (428 to 437), the phase 3 decisions (438 to 471) and this item. The merge dialog half of decision 403 is now built (decision 451). Decision 448 is a deliberate deviation from the brief and joins the list above. The roadmap entry `projects-ui` is `complete-with-blockers`: PJ3 passed in four layouts with simulated evidence, but the vision checks that brief section 13 requires were not run (ENV-CLAUDE), and no single four-worker browser matrix run was green on this machine (6 and then 20 timeouts in the heaviest skeleton journeys under external load, each passing alone in the previous commit's time).
473. (D30) Placement question set p-v1: the `pick_model` criteria are the model menu descriptions keyed by model id, and the instructions are fixed texts: isolation `Choose how this new thread works: in its own worktree ending in a pull request, or directly on main.`, pick_model `Choose the model that should carry this thread end to end.`, effort `Choose the reasoning effort this thread needs.` and device `Choose the device that should run this thread.`. Resolution takes the option with the highest probability (brief 10) and breaks exact ties with Jev's `choice` (decision 479).
474. (D30a) When Call B (the device) fails after Call A succeeded, every field that is not fixed uses the deterministic fallback, and Call A's record stays in the placement's Jev calls.
475. (D30b) When no Jev call is needed because every remaining choice has one option, the record says `source: 'fixed'` and its `fixed` list names only the fields the caller fixed.
476. (D50) The coordinator's placement override summaries read `Model changed from {from} to {to}.` and `Effort changed from {from} to {to}.` (likewise `Isolation` and `Device`), one sentence per change joined with spaces, then ` Note: {note}`; a restart adds `Restarted as {newId}.` after the changes (decision 480). The values are the stored ids, not labels.
477. (D249) The `placement-state-v1` packet holds the title, the task, the coordinator's note (for a restart, the owner's note), the project's active threads oldest first (title, isolation, device name, model label, effort and reserved paths; the newest 50 are kept) and the newest 8 placement overrides as one sentence per change, newest first (at most 32), with the stored values, for example `model changed from deep to swift for 'Add B' (restart)`. The whole document is redacted first and only then is the task cut to 6,000 characters with `\n[Task shortened.]` and the note to 600, so a cut can never split a secret before the redactor saw it. Its default isolation is the effective one (decision 419). Over 12,000 estimated tokens it clears reserved paths thread by thread, then drops active threads, then override sentences, oldest first each, measuring again after every step; a packet still too large records `state-too-large` and places with the fallback. The packet is built only when a call has questions, so a placement without questions never falls back for its size.
478. (D250) When Jev answers `main` together with a model that no device able to work on main can run, code changes neither the isolation nor the model: every field that is not fixed uses the deterministic fallback with `{ kind: 'incompatible-answer', message: 'no device can run the chosen model on main' }`, and Call A's record stays. It cannot happen while main isolation is gated (decision 294). Main isolation exists since Projects phase 6 (decision 525).
479. (D251) Placement answers: the option with the highest probability wins; an exact tie goes to Jev's `choice` when it is among the tied options, else to the first tied option in criteria order. `probabilities` holds the answered distribution per asked question (`isolation`, `pick_model`, `effort`, `device`), only on `source: 'jev'` records; fallback records carry none, also after a failed Call B (decision 474). Jev records list the chosen isolation's model options and the chosen model's devices exactly as the fallback does (decision 339), through one shared record builder. The placed result carries the running-limit flag (decision 336), so a start on a full device still queues. An already cancelled start throws before any call; an answer that cannot be resolved (missing, or an unknown effort or isolation) is `invalid-response` and falls back; errors other than Jev errors and the key source's plain 503 (`credential-unavailable`, its message kept) propagate. The new placement texts live with the other placement constants (decision 338).
480. (D252) Placement overrides (brief 10, 11 and 12.3). The hub keeps them append-only in `project-placement-overrides` (operations `override-add`, a write, and `overrides-recent`, a read of 1 to 100 newest first). Only the thread's owner records them (403 `Only the thread owner can record its placement overrides.`), the project must exist (404), and an id seen before answers with its first record when it describes the same change, else 409 `This override id was already used for a different change.`. Override and coordinator event ids derive from the request's client id, so every step of a retried request is idempotent; notes are trimmed, redacted and cut to 400 characters. From the next turn: an isolation or device is 400 `From the next turn, only the model and effort can change.`; an ended thread 409 with the ended-thread text (decision 288); an unknown model 409 `Choose a model from the configuration.`; another runtime 409 `From the next turn, the model must use the same runtime.`; the new model must also pass this thread's placement candidate check (running limits ignored), else 409 with its refusal. Only fields that change are recorded, so a request with nothing to change, like a retry after success, is a 200 no-op. The order is the hub record, the coordinator's summary (decision 476), then the owner applies it: the placement's model and efforts change at once (the effective effort mapped for the new model), a `thread-placement` ledger event is appended and the index label changes, while `source`, `fixed`, the probabilities and the Jev calls keep the original decision (the hub log tells the change). It is applied outside the thread's step chain, so the request never waits for a running turn: that turn keeps its model, and the next launch takes the new one, keeping the account while it stays eligible and resuming the session (decision 252). A restart is refused with 409 `This thread has an open pull request.` (brief 12.3), `This thread already published to main.` or `This thread was already restarted.`, except for a retry. The new thread is placed first, on the coordinator's device, with the request's fields fixed and the owner's note as the packet's note, so a refusal leaves the old thread untouched and a retry finds the same new thread. Then the record (the fields that differ), then the old thread ends with exactly `Restarted as {newId}.` (a live one stops without telling the coordinator) and its worktree and local branch are removed, except for done threads (cleaned when they concluded) and discarded ones; a restarted thread counts as discarded, so Discard is neither offered nor repeated. The coordinator hears of the new thread through the usual owner-started line and of the old one through one summary ending in `Restarted as {newId}.` and the note. The route answers 200 with the new thread id, and the thread view's `canOverride` says which modes are open, with the restart refusal as its reason.
481. (D253) Jev placement in the daemon. Placement reads the hub (thread pages and recent overrides) and this device's own threads, whose copies replace the hub's because those lag one publish, and asks Jev through the decisions client with the configured Jev model and the start's cancellation. Without a Jev client every question fails as `no-key`, so in-process tests and devices without Jev place with the fallback, recording `{ kind: 'no-key', message: 'no key configured' }` with the summary `placed without Jev: no key configured`; this supersedes decision 289, whose `not-enabled` record is removed. The packet's active threads are the project's non-terminal threads oldest first (created time, then id) with the roster device name (the id when unknown), the index model label and effort, and no reserved paths until main isolation exists; its overrides are the hub's newest 8 with titles from the same thread indexes (`(unknown thread)` when an index is missing). A hub outage leaves the hub part out instead of refusing the start; other hub errors propagate. Refusals are computed before the packet is gathered, and a placement with all four fields fixed reads nothing. The phase gates (decision 294) are unchanged. Since Projects phase 6 the packet carries each active thread's reserved paths from the hub (decision 525).
482. (D254) PJ4 and PJ4b integration details. With main isolation gated (decision 294) Call A never asks the isolation, so PJ4 asserts the question keys from the phase-gate value (`pick_model` and `effort` now, the isolation as well once main isolation exists), and the Leave git project's missing isolation question is meaningful only then; Call B is never sent while placement stays on this device, so the pure tests cover both. The planned restart sentence `model changed from deep to swift` contradicted its own sequence: the second next-turn override had already moved the thread to `deep-lite`, and `from` is the current value, so the record and the packet say `model changed from deep-lite to swift for 'Add search' (restart)`. The restart's own placement already sees the earlier next-turn sentences, and the restart sentence reaches the placements after it. The project fixture can save a Jev key (a random fixture value, added to the leak check), and its second runtime is a FakeRuntime with its own display name, so the no-candidate refusal names both runtimes. Since Projects phase 6 Call A asks the isolation too where main is allowed (decision 525), and the PJ4b browser journey shows its bars.
483. (D255) Thread page placement interface (brief 12.3 and 9.8). The header holds Why (pressed while its panel is open, always shown) and Override (shown while either mode is open) before Stop, Discard and Allow 10 more turns, icon-only below 560 px of page width (decision 456). A `source: 'fallback'` record with an error shows the brief's chip `Placed without Jev: {error.message}` last in the meta row, warn-toned and wrapping, because the reason can be 300 characters. A restarted thread's reason links the new thread (the `Restarted as ` prefix joined the texts the browser reads back from the server, decision 439, with unchanged bytes). The Why panel (`Why this placement`, the thread title as eyebrow) shows the placement source and the fixed fields with the fallback error, then Isolation, Model, Effort and Device, each with a source label (`Jev`, `fixed`, `only option` for a question Jev was not asked, `fallback rule` or `changed by you`), Jev's probabilities as bars, highest first, with the value the thread holds now marked, the requested and effective effort when they differ (`{requested} → {effective} (nearest effort this model supports)`), and for Model and Device the excluded options with their reasons (without bars, the eligible ones with the chosen one checked); then the account, the Jev calls (`Placement · {model} · {tokens} tokens · {ms} ms`, or a sentence saying that no Jev call was needed or no Jev answer was used) and the decision time. A next-turn override keeps Jev's probabilities (decision 480), so a current value below Jev's top one reads `changed by you` with its own bar marked; a field changed while fixed still reads `fixed`. The Override modal offers From the next turn and Restart with these choices, each with a line saying what it does or why it is refused (the ended-thread text, or the server's restart reason such as `This thread has an open pull request.`), and opens on From the next turn when that is allowed. Its fields start at the thread's current choices: the next turn offers Model (the enabled entries of the thread's runtime) and Effort; a restart adds Automatic to every field plus Isolation and Device, whose Main and other-device options are disabled with the gate sentences (decision 445). Effort lists the model's efforts plus the thread's requested one, shown as `{effort} (runs as {mapped})` when the model does not offer it, so a modal left alone never sends the mapped effort as a change. The next turn sends only what differs, and Apply stays disabled until something does; a restart sends every field not on Automatic, so by default all four are fixed and no Jev call is made. Apply is danger-styled for a restart, which stops the thread and removes its worktree, and refusals stay in the dialog. After a next-turn Apply the toast says `The next turn uses your choices.` and the page reads the view at once; after a restart the page opens the new thread with `The thread restarted with your choices.`. Conversation Why panels now label placement Jev calls `Placement` (only the label changed).
484. (D256) Browser fixture for PJ4b. The fixture server's fake Jev answers `placement-state-v1` packets: isolation `worktree` 0.8, the first model 0.7 and effort `high` 0.6, the rest of each distribution shared equally, and the device question with the hub's own device; a task containing `PJ4b fallback` gets HTTP 401. With `--projects` the hub saves a fixture Jev key at start, so every thread placement on those servers asks this fake Jev and PJ3's placements now record `jev` (the PJ3 journeys build their expectations from the views), and Claude discovery also offers Opus as a second model of the same runtime. A fourth project, `Projects placement`, holds two threads started through the real start path: `Tidy the README wording`, placed by Jev, and `Check the README links`, placed with `{ kind: 'auth', message: 'authentication failed' }`, both idle after one progress turn without a pull request, so the PJ3 counts on `Projects fixture` stay as they were; the sidebar gains one row on these servers. PJ4b screenshots are named after the journey (`PJ4b-<step>-<layout>.png`), so PJ3's file names are unchanged. The browser journey checks the next launch through the thread view after `Report · turn 2`; the next packet's override sentences and the removal of the restarted thread's worktree are checked by the integration test only.
485. (D257) The builder's second visual review, standing in for the vision checks that did not run, led to these fixes: (1) panels on Projects pages truncate their eyebrow (the thread title in Why, the project name in Notebook) and keep the panel title whole, because a long thread title pushed `Why this placement` under Close and scrolled the heading sideways; conversation panels are unchanged. (2) The sidebar's `{n} running` counts live work only (preparing, running, publishing), so an idle or waiting thread is never called running (brief 12.1), while the project page's Running section still lists every non-terminal thread not in review, as brief 12.2 names it, so its header can count more than the sidebar; the thread page tells the sidebar to read again when its state changes. (3) The Why panel lists the other options of a field the owner fixed as plain muted rows instead of red `not chosen` exclusions, and its decision time uses the Projects timestamp. (4) A radio reached with the keyboard in Project settings and Override rings its option with the app's focus ring, because the app drops every input's outline and a native radio cannot draw a round ring. (5) Danger buttons (Stop, Discard, Remove token and a restart's Apply) take the theme's text color for accent fills (white on the dark theme's salmon read at about 2.6:1) and a deeper hover, and a busy one reads disabled. (6) The PJ3 journey waits for the coordinator to answer the merge and stay idle before the `concluded` capture, every capture also requires open panel headings not to scroll sideways, the thread journey opens Why on the long-titled thread, and PJ4b checks that the sidebar shows no running count for idle threads.
486. (D258) The phase 4 verification ran the full browser matrix twice. Round 1 used the configured four workers; round 2, the second and last full run, used `--workers=2`, because no four-worker matrix run had been green on this machine (phase 3's two runs and phase 4's first), and since the J11 cause was fixed (decisions 470 and 471) every remaining failure in those runs had been documented as load noise. Nothing about the tests changed: specs, assertions, timeouts and retries are the configured ones, and the configuration keeps four workers. Four-worker coverage is round 1 (194 of 196, both failures load noise); the committed vision records and matrix screenshots come from round 2 (196 of 196). A failure counts as load noise only when its test passes alone in all four layouts in the previous commit's time; a first pair of alone timings about 1 s apart is sampled again in the opposite order before it is called either way.
487. (D259) Phase 4 copies the decisions it implements as 473 to 487 in the format of decision 392: the placement decisions deferred until now (473 to 476), the phase 4 decisions (477 to 486) and this item. Decision 481 supersedes decision 289, and decisions 289, 456 and 459 now point to their phase 4 successors. No phase 4 decision is a new deviation from the brief. The roadmap entry `projects-placement` is `complete-with-blockers`: PJ4 and PJ4b pass with simulated evidence (a scripted Jev fetch in the integration tests and the fixture server's fake Jev in the browser), but the vision checks that brief section 13 requires were not run (ENV-CLAUDE), live Jev placement was not run (ENV-JEV), and the only fully green browser matrix ran on two workers (decision 486).
488. (D40) Deliberate deviation from brief 11, whose peer routes include `POST /api/mesh/projects/:id/threads` (start a placed thread on this device) and `POST /api/mesh/projects/:id/coordinator/events` (deliver events to the coordinator device): neither exists on any device, and both answer 404 `Project operation not found.`. They would be background calls between devices, and peer authentication needs a signed browser session token that background work never has (decision 489). Thread starts, thread commands and coordinator events travel through the hub relay `project-envelopes` instead, with a durable outbox on the sending device and polling on the target (decisions 492 to 495). The brief's other peer routes exist as routes the interface reaches through the owner proxy (decision 498).
489. (D41) Project peer routes authenticate with the generic peer check (`peerLoginAuthenticated`, the browser's session forwarded by the owner proxy), without binding the session to a project or thread; the receiver then serves only a thread it owns or a coordinator it runs (decision 498).
490. (D43) After Move coordinator here the new device starts a fresh coordinator session whose recap starts empty, so its first turn is built from the hub's notebook and indexes; the former device keeps its ledger files, drops its coordinator state and checks the assignment before every turn (decisions 501 and 503).
491. (D90) Delivered envelopes are deleted when the target acknowledges them; idempotency stays on the target, through its seen set and idempotent handlers (decisions 492 and 495). Mail older than 14 days and reservations released or expired more than a day ago are to be pruned on write; that half arrives with mail and reservations in main isolation (phase 6). That half was built in Projects phase 6 (decision 523).
492. (D260) The relay has three hub operations on the `envelopes` collection: `envelope-put` (write) answers `{ id, stored }`, with `stored: false` for an identical retry (compared with the revision zeroed; the hub sets revision 1, decision 239); `envelopes-pending` (read, admitted outside the lifecycle gate like every read, decision 471) answers `{ records, more }`; `envelope-ack` (write) answers `{ id, deleted }` and takes no time, because the row is deleted in the same transaction (decision 491). Pending envelopes are selected by target device in SQL (decision 295) and sorted in relay order. A page is cut at 100 envelopes or when the next one would pass 1 MiB of serialized JSON, always holding at least one: a thread start (a task of up to 20,000 characters, queued messages, the placement record) or a user message can be 20 to 300 KB, and 100 of them would pass the member transport's 2 MiB reply cap and fail every poll for good. Hub refusals: a put by a device other than the envelope's source 403 `Only the source device can send its envelopes.`, an unknown project 404 `Project not found.`, a set `deliveredAt` 400, the same id with other content 409 `This envelope id was already used for a different message.`; a pending read for another device 403 `Only the target device can read its envelopes.` (otherwise any device could read another's thread tasks); an acknowledgement by a device other than the target 403 `Only the target device can acknowledge an envelope.`, and an unknown id counts as already acknowledged. The member checks every reply: the put and acknowledgement ids, and every pending record for the requested target, unique, in relay order (source, project, then sequence, ids compared by code point), and never an empty page that announces more. `HubDatabase.delete(namespace, id)` is one `DELETE` statement with both ids validated, so it joins an enclosing transaction. The relay route wakes the hub's inbox after replying when an `envelope-put` targets the hub's own device, stored or not (a retry wakes it again). The hub does not check that the target device exists or is still authorized: envelopes for a device revoked later stay on the hub, and a coordinator move retargets the coordinator events among them (decision 502).
493. (D261) Outbox. Enqueueing returns the outbox entry: the target is `coordinator` exactly for coordinator events, and a thread start goes to its thread's owner device. Envelope ids derive from the project, the source device, the kind and a key (the event id, the thread id or the command id), so the same message enqueued again repeats its envelope: while it waits the outbox returns the waiting entry (other content under the same key is refused, 409), once delivered the hub stores it again only if the target already deleted it, and the target's seen set runs it once. `seq.json` (`project-outbox-seq-v1`, the last number used, never below the highest waiting entry) is written before the entry, so a crash leaves a gap and never reuses a number. A coordinator event resolves its target from the coordinator assignment at the first send attempt (with no assignment, this device, whose own inbox then queues the event, decision 241), and the resolved device is written into the entry before the put: a lost put reply followed by a coordinator move must not put the same id to another device, which the hub would refuse with 409 and the event would be dropped. Entries written before a move and never attempted still go to the new device; an event already sent to the old device is forwarded by it (decision 503). A drain walks projects in id order and each project's entries in sequence order, deleting each file once the hub holds the envelope. Enqueueing, a pulse and the start drain at once (an explicit trigger replaces a scheduled retry), and a drain requested while one runs shares one follow-up drain, so nothing enqueued meanwhile waits for a timer. A retryable failure stops the whole drain (such failures are hub-wide: unreachable, maintenance, authentication) and schedules one retry after 10, 20, 40 and then 60 seconds (500, 1,000 and then 2,000 ms on the browser fixture); a successful drain resets the schedule. The retry timer runs even when periodic timers are off, like the index publisher's retry: it finishes failed work and does not poll. A drain never rejects, and an envelope stored for this device itself wakes its own inbox.
494. (D262) Both ends of the relay use the rule of decision 342: a 4xx answer other than 401, 408 and 429 can never succeed, and anything else (no status, 5xx, an unreachable hub, a protocol error) is retried. The outbox drops a refused entry and continues with the next (only a deleted project or a programming error gets there; an undeliverable entry at the head would otherwise hold its project's queue forever). The inbox records a refused envelope as seen and acknowledges it (a command for a thread or project this device does not hold, a reused message id); a retryable handler failure holds the rest of that source's project sequence until the next poll while other sequences continue, and another page is read in the same poll only while the previous page acknowledged something.
495. (D263) The inbox's seen set is per device, `<home>/projects/inbox-seen.json` (`project-inbox-seen-v1`, the newest 2,000 ids, written before the acknowledgement); a seen id is only acknowledged again. The inbox polls every 3 seconds only when timers are periodic (the first poll at start); otherwise a pulse and the relay's wake-up poll, so a daemon without periodic timers sends no background hub requests. The Projects service owns the outbox and the inbox: a pulse ends with a drain and a poll, the start drains once (entries left by an earlier run) and starts the inbox, a relay wake-up polls at once, waiting for idle covers both, and closing stops the inbox first and closes the outbox last (its entries stay on disk). Handlers: a coordinator event needs its project (404 otherwise, so it is dropped and no coordinator state appears) and enters this device's coordinator queue, which dedupes by event id, or is handed over when the coordinator moved (decision 503); a thread command needs its thread here (404 otherwise) and is applied once per command id (the thread-local `seenCommands`, the newest 200), and a dispatch runs its preparation and first turn in the background so the inbox never waits on a turn; a thread start is handled as decision 496 describes.
496. (D264) Remote starts and the queue across devices. The device gate of decision 294 is open (the gate itself stays for tests): every online device with the project path and an eligible account is a candidate, and Call B asks for the device when more than one qualifies; a fixed device that cannot run threads gets the placement refusal (for example `No device can run any enabled model: Studio: offline.`), not the phase text. A start placed on another device sends the thread (owner-local fields empty, its owner the placed device) as a `thread-start` envelope, and admission counts it as a pending dispatch toward both limits until the thread's hub index shows a state other than `queued`, or for at most 2 minutes; one rule covers fresh starts and dispatches of queued threads, and a queued start registers none. `thread-start-receipt-v1` gains an optional `started { state, stateReason?, placement }`, so a retried start (an MCP retry of `jevellan_thread_start`, a retried New thread) of a thread on another device answers that thread, with the state of its hub index once published, instead of creating a second one. The owner device creates a relayed thread once (an existing thread.json is a repeat) with labels from its configuration menu and the hub account list, prepares it in the background unless it is `queued`, and sends no owner-started line (the coordinator device sent it); a start for another owner or project is refused 409 `This thread starts on another device.` and dropped (decision 494). The queue sweep runs only on the coordinator device (any device before an assignment; none while the hub is unreachable), over the project's queued threads here and, from the hub indexes, on other devices, oldest first by creation time then id; a thread elsewhere gets a `dispatch` thread command with a derived command id and a pending dispatch, and is skipped while that counts. The sweep covers projects with queued threads here or a coordinator here, and a relayed coordinator event schedules its project's sweep, because work on another device rested. The sweep reads the hub's indexes only for projects that may have queued threads elsewhere (each project coordinated here once after start, then while a queued start sent from here has not been seen leaving the queue or a remote queued index remains), so an idle sweep costs no hub request; a start its owner never publishes keeps its project read on every sweep.
497. (D265) Delivery across devices. A coordinator event is queued on this device when it holds the coordinator assignment or none exists (decision 241), and relayed to the coordinator otherwise; while coordinator events already wait in this device's outbox, later ones follow them, to keep the order; while the hub is unreachable the last assignment read decides, and one never read leaves the event to the relay, which resolves the coordinator when it sends. Commands and starts for another device are relayed; with no relay that is a programming error. Retries repeat their derived command ids: coordinator messages derive from the project, thread and message id (the message id already derives from the turn and input), coordinator stops from the turn, answered questions from the question id for stop and allow-turns, and dispatches as decision 496 says; other commands are fresh. A message, stop or allow-turns for a thread that is not here uses its hub index (404 when absent, in another project or owned by this device): the sender rules apply to the index (a coordinator message to an attached thread, or any message to an ended one, 409), the delivery is `queued` (`interrupting` when the index says `running` and an interrupt was asked), never `started`, with `repeated: false`, and a stop of an ended thread changes nothing. The `stop` command gains an optional `notify`, because an owner's stop tells the coordinator (decision 264). On the owner device a relayed `message` command takes the same message path (sender rules, idempotent by message id, and an owner message is echoed to the coordinator as `thread-user-message` exactly once, by the owner device); `dispatch` and `allow-turns` run in the background of the inbox (allow-turns waits on the thread's step chain). `jevellan_pr_status` for a thread elsewhere reads the repository from its pull request URL, falling back to this device's checkout remote; decision 398 was already built (decision 417).
498. (D266) Thread and coordinator requests from the interface reach the device that holds the work. Thread routes resolve the thread's device (thread.json here first, then the hub index); another device is looked up in the roster: missing or revoked 409 `The device that runs this thread is no longer in this mesh.`, offline 409 `{name} is offline. This thread waits there until it is back.` (refused at once instead of a 20 s proxy timeout on every poll), otherwise the owner proxy forwards the request. Coordinator routes with the assignment elsewhere: a missing, revoked or offline device 409 with the brief's notice `The coordinator lives on {name}, which is offline.` (decision 245), otherwise forwarded, the chat stream included; before any assignment the request runs here (decision 241). The proxy's texts for these requests are `Can't reach {name}. This thread's work stays on that device.`, `Can't reach {name}. The coordinator stays on that device.`, `The thread's device returned an unexpected redirect.` and `The coordinator's device returned an unexpected redirect.`; the conversation and login texts are unchanged (now one table). The peer receiver `/api/mesh/projects/<suffix>` runs before the cookie check: browser headers 403 `Use the device connection for this request.`; only the thread and coordinator routes are served, and other paths and a known path with another method answer 404 `Project operation not found.`; then the generic peer check (decision 489), else 401 `Sign in to Jevellan.`; the token joins the redactor; the resource must be here (404 `This thread was not found.` or `The coordinator does not run on this device.`); then the same handler runs, which never forwards again, and the chat stream re-checks the session with the stream outage tolerance. New thread from a device that is not the coordinator's is forwarded as `POST /api/mesh/projects/:id/coordinator/threads`, because every start runs on the coordinator device (decision 245) while the brief's `POST /api/mesh/projects/:id/threads` stays absent (decision 488). A restart override whose coordinator is on another device runs on the thread's device (refusals, ending the old thread, the record) and asks the coordinator device for the new thread through that same route, with the owner's session and the device the session belongs to (so a hub page restarting a member thread whose coordinator is the hub verifies), then keeps its own start receipt with `started`, so a retry passes the refusals the restart itself caused; `thread-create-request-v1` gains an optional `note` for the restart's owner note.
499. (D267) One hub read serves the Projects list: the `work` collection's read operation `work-summaries` (`after?`) answers at most 100 `project-work-summary-v1` records and `next`: per hub project in id order its name, the counts (moved to the core rules, over every thread index and every open question) and the assigned coordinator `{ deviceId, device: { name, status, revoked } | null, state }`, with presence from the roster the hub store is given (unknown devices read null) and `state` the published coordinator state, null when none was published or the status belongs to a former coordinator device. The client reads every page (members check ascending ids and `next`). The Projects list reads only this, with the row rules of decision 470 (this device: the local state; an unknown, offline or revoked device: `offline`; otherwise the published state, `idle` before any), so a member's list costs one hub request per poll, besides the session check every browser request makes; before, it made three hub requests per project on every poll (a gap recorded in phase 3).
500. (D268) The published coordinator status carries `lastEventId` (optional, the coordinator ledger's last id when published). The project view's `lastEventId` (the chat history bound for the withdrawn-question toasts) is this device's coordinator ledger when the coordinator runs here or none is assigned, otherwise the assigned device's published `lastEventId` (0 when it published none), because the chat streams from that device: a page on another device would otherwise toast every withdrawn question of the history when it opens. With the device gate open (decision 496), the PJ4b browser journey's Why panel lists the other devices with their real reasons (`Browser member: not set up for this project`, `Offline fixture: offline`) instead of the phase text. A test-only member fixture joins a simulated member to the project fixture over HTTP: its own home, daemon, FakeRuntime and account `acc_member`, a clone with the GitHub remote, a browser session and a heartbeat.
501. (D269) Move coordinator here. One rule drives both the work view's `canMoveHere` and the route's refusal: the move is allowed when the coordinator's device is missing from the roster, revoked or offline (no heartbeat for 10 minutes), or when the status that device published says `idle` or `unavailable`; a device that published nothing yet, and a status a former coordinator device left, read as idle, as the chip shows them, so the work view now takes the coordinator state, session and `lastEventId` only from the assigned device's own status. A stale device keeps its state (decision 435) and may refuse. The refusal is 409 `The coordinator is working on {device}. Try again when it is idle.`. `canMoveHere` is false before any assignment and on the coordinator's own device. The route `POST /api/projects/:id/coordinator/move` runs on the device the owner uses and is never forwarded (the peer receiver answers 404 for it, as for any path that is neither a thread nor a coordinator route); it takes `empty-request-v1` and answers 200 with the project view, and a hub outage during it is reported as retryable, like the client-id requests, because a repeat completes the first. Moves are serialized per project (concurrent moves share one, and an assignment check waits for a running move, then reads): compare-and-swap on the observed revision, read again after a lost race, three attempts; before any assignment it assigns this device at revision 0 as a first message would (decision 241), with no notice and no state. After a move this device writes its coordinator state as idle, with no session, no failed turns and the queue it kept (events it received while it coordinated earlier and never handed over; nothing else carries on), appends the info notice `The coordinator moved to {name}.`, publishes its status at once and starts a turn only when events wait. When the assignment already names this device the setup runs only if the coordinator state is absent (the reply of a completed move was lost), so a repeat never writes a second notice or resets a queue. The move also marks the project for one read of the remote queue and schedules its sweep (decision 496).
502. (D270) A move retargets the relay. When the coordinator assignment changes device, the hub retargets in the same transaction every `coordinator-event` envelope of the project still waiting for the former device to the new one (revision bumped), so events relayed to a device that is gone follow the coordinator instead of waiting for that device forever. A sender that retries such an envelope after a lost reply is refused 409 (the stored copy now differs), a permanent refusal that drops its outbox entry while the hub keeps the retargeted copy. A former device whose acknowledgement races the move gets 403 for it; its next poll no longer lists the envelope, and its own handover forwards the event, which the new coordinator dedupes by event id. Thread starts and commands are never retargeted.
503. (D271) Handover on the former coordinator device. Triggers: (a) the assignment check, now read twice per turn, at its start and again after the preparation just before the launch (the preparation's hub reads leave a window in which a move can be accepted while the published status still says idle); (b) the start: the daemon reads the assignment once for every coordinator with saved state here, whatever its queue, and an unreachable hub leaves the unread ones to the next sweep only, so idle sweeps read nothing; (c) the sweep, for a stalled coordinator only (two failed turns, events waiting, no owner retry pending, nothing running or scheduled): no turn would start and reach the check, and moving away from a failing coordinator is a likely move, so each sweep reads its assignment (one hub read per sweep, in that state only); (d) the inbox: a relayed coordinator event for a project whose assignment names another device hands over with that event appended (an unreachable hub fails the handler as retryable, decision 494). A handover marks the coordinator moved (no start, sweep or scheduled turn afterwards), clears a scheduled start, stops a running turn and waits for it (its batch counts as delivered, as after Stop, decision 395, and the ledger records it interrupted), forwards every undelivered queue event and then the new event through the outbox to the coordinator (resolved when sent, decision 493; envelope ids derive from this device and the event id, so the new coordinator dedupes), records their ids in the coordinator-local `forwardedEventIds` (optional, the newest 500), and only then removes the coordinator state. The coordinator ledger stays as history and coordinator-local.json keeps the last delivered turn, so a ledger the coordinator returns to never repeats a turn number. Forwarded ids no longer count as received there: should the coordinator return before another device delivered them, they are taken again (and leave the list); otherwise the history built from the ledger would drop them as repeats. Repeating a handover sends only the new event. A coordinator that moved is replaced when next used, so one that returns starts clean. After a move the new device reads its inbox at once, so envelopes the hub retargeted (decision 502) arrive without waiting for a poll. Decision 401 needed no change: both checks postpone while the hub is unreachable.
504. (D272) Move in the interface. The project menu shows Move coordinator here only when the view's `canMoveHere`; the brief's offline notice carries the button at its end, and the button wraps under the sentence on narrow pages; the New thread dialog shows the same notice with the button instead of a proxy error (decision 245), and after the move its form starts the thread on this device. The busy text is `Moving…`. The move answers the new view, which the page shows at once, then reads again. A refused move is shown on the page as an error line (never the app's global 409 reload) until the coordinator's device changes or the menu offers the move again; in the dialog it uses the dialog's own error line. The chat streams one device's ledger, the coordinator's (this device's before any assignment); when a work view names another device, the page closes the stream, clears the shown events, the cursor and the withdrawal bound (set to the new view's `lastEventId`), and opens the stream again without a cursor, because the new device's ledger ids restart and overlap the old ones and an open stream to the old device never fails by itself.
505. (D273) Hub outages at a thread's turn boundary. A turn launch needs the hub (the project, the account list and the account choice read or write it), as conversation steps do, and a member's browser needs the hub to sign every request, so the build design's plan for PJ5 (two messages sent from the member's page while the hub is down, both turns running) cannot happen; its intent stays: turns that are running finish, their events wait in the outbox in order and arrive once each after reconnect. A thread whose next turn, for waiting messages, cannot start because the hub is unreachable rests (a live state becomes `idle`, a rest state is kept) with the stable reason `Waiting for the hub. This thread continues when it's back.`, writes one error notice with the hub error per wait (not again at each failed sweep) and tells the coordinator nothing, because it is no failure; the sweep that starts threads waiting for a free slot starts these once the hub answers. Before, such a thread rested with `This step failed: Can't reach the hub ... This will continue when it's back.`, sent a `failed` interruption, and nothing ever resumed its messages. Without waiting messages (a task turn, a verification retry, a publication) the existing failure path stays, so the coordinator still hears of it; a launch whose own hub reads fail after the turn's state change still ends as a failed turn. A report followed by waiting messages is told with the step's last report, as before, so a thread resting on the hub has told nothing of that step yet.
506. (D274) A thread page right after a remote start. A thread placed on another device exists there, and in the hub index, only once that device read its start from the hub, so its page, opened right after New thread, a restart or a chat link, answered 404 and showed `This thread was not found.` until its next read 10 s later. The page now treats a 404 before any view as still starting while the creation time encoded in the thread id is within 2 minutes (either side, for clock skew between devices): it keeps the loading line and reads again every 1.5 s. Older or foreign ids show the error as before. There is no server change: the coordinator device knows only a pending dispatch, not a view.
507. (D275) PJ5 harness and browser seeding. The member fixture gains its account id (`acc_member`) and a lost-reply hook (a hub request reaches the hub, then the member sees an outage). The member's account stays the per-device `acc_member`, checked on the member only: the interface's add route makes per-device accounts only for Codex subscriptions, and a shared credential for the fake runtime has no delivery, so the account is not added through the member's interface as the build design planned. The lost acknowledgement the design planned is the member's lost `envelope-put` reply (the hub, the target there, acknowledges locally); the hub restart is the test's last step, because the fixture's restart listens on a new port, which strands the member's hub address; hub and member coordinator turns say `Noted.` unless scripted, because an unscripted turn fails and two failures stall a coordinator. `scripts/test-server.mjs --projects`: the member runs its Projects loops periodically (it needed a pulse before), signs in to `acc_projects_codex` on its own (a fixture `auth.json` in its per-device home) and has no Claude account, so Claude placements never qualify it and the PJ3 and PJ4b journeys keep their placements; a thread task marked `PJ5` reads the README (one Read row) and reports progress. The first PJ5 browser journey starts the thread with Device `Browser member` (fixed, so no Jev device question) and asserts through a MutationObserver that `This thread was not found.` never shows; the second runs last, because it moves the `Projects offline` coordinator for the rest of the server's life, and reads the hub's name from the work view.
508. (D276) A thread page whose device cannot answer before any view. The hub refuses a thread view read with 409 while the thread's device is offline or left the mesh, and answers 502 when it cannot reach it; opened in that state (a member laptop asleep while the owner uses the phone) the page was a back link, an empty title and a red error. Now such a failure before any view (409 shown as a notice, 502 as an error line) makes the page read the project's work view once and show the thread's title and last state from its index row, which the hub holds; the reason shows under the header and the page keeps reading every 10 s, so the thread appears when its device answers. Any later failure before a view keeps the title and reads as an error. Other failures (404 after the start window of decision 506, 401, 5xx other than 502) are unchanged. Once a view was shown, a refusal still reads as the existing notice above the stale view, with the actions left enabled (their refusals stay local).
509. (D277) Banner spacing and the move button. Page banners on Projects pages (notices and error lines, including the refused-move line) take a 10 px top margin and none below; on two-column pages, where the tab bar is hidden, the last banner before it keeps 10 px below too, so the chat's divider never runs into a notice. Move coordinator here keeps its width while busy: both labels share one grid cell and the inactive one is hidden and `aria-hidden`, so the notice does not reflow on phones when `Moving…` replaces the label. The offline notice as the New thread form's first child keeps the dialog's padding above and the fields' 12 px below instead of the shared notice margin.
510. (D278) Requests that lose their client, and requests in flight at shutdown (pre-existing and separable from the Projects work). Cause, reproduced on the real browser fixture: a daemon request enters the lifecycle gate before it awaits other work (on a member the session check reaches the hub), and only then reads its JSON body; a client that left during that wait has already destroyed the request, so its body never ends and its `aborted` and `error` events have passed. The body read waited forever and the activity was never released: the member could never enter maintenance (updates and removal wait for an idle gate) and its close failed with `Application cleanup did not complete.` / `Release active work or maintenance before closing the lifecycle gate.` (60 partial authenticated `POST /api/improver` requests to a fixture member left 50 activities held forever; the first phase 5 browser matrix run leaked one at the end of the phone-dark hub boundaries journey, held 16 minutes until shutdown). Fix: the body read refuses a request that is already destroyed, and one that closes before its end, with the existing `The request was interrupted.` (400). A second, smaller race: a request still in flight when the daemon closes held the gate when the application reached the gate's close (a member's close takes about 1 ms). The gate can now wait until no admitted work remains, and the application waits for that for up to 5 s before closing the gate, so admitted requests (their clients gone once the listeners closed) finish first, and work that stays stuck still fails the close as before. `scripts/test-server.mjs` now closes each listener before its application, as the daemon does (the member's listener, the member, the hub's listener, the hub). Regression tests in `tests/lifecycle.test.ts` (a destroyed request releases its activity and maintenance is available; a closing daemon lets an admitted request finish) each fail against the old build. The fixture server close failures recorded in Projects phases 3 and 4, whose cause was not identified then, likely share these causes (phase 4's leaked root held only start-time directories, as an improver server does, whose interface centers on `POST /api/improver`; phase 3's ended right after J11, which ends on the member's interface); that is not proven for those runs. Projects phase 8 found the remaining cause, Playwright's process-group SIGTERM reaching the fixture server's own child processes, and gave the server a process group of its own (decision 559).
511. (D279) Phase 5 copies the decisions it implements as 488 to 511 in the format of decision 392: the mesh decisions deferred until now (488 to 491; only the envelope half of 491 is built, and its mail and reservation half arrives with main isolation), the phase 5 decisions (492 to 510) and this item. Decision 488 is a deliberate deviation from brief 11 and joins the list above. Decisions 294, 295, 371, 373, 417, 420, 445, 452 and 459 now point to their phase 5 successors. Brief section 4 decision 7 (a thread's needs-decision report creates the owner question directly when the coordinator's device is offline) is not built: such a report waits until a coordinator runs, and Move coordinator here is the way out. That is a known gap, not a deviation. The roadmap entry `projects-mesh` is `complete-with-blockers`: PJ5 passes with simulated evidence, both devices being daemons on this machine over local HTTP (no second physical device, DEVICE), with scripted and fixture Jev answers (ENV-JEV), FakeRuntime turns and the fake GitHub server, but the vision checks that brief section 13 requires were not run (ENV-CLAUDE), and the owner-question fallback for an offline coordinator device is missing. Decision 512 built that fallback on 2026-10-05, and decisions 513 to 515 closed three more of the phase 5 known gaps (see Current state).
512. (D280) The owner-device decision fallback (brief section 4 decision 7). While the coordinator's device is away (missing from the roster, revoked, or offline with no heartbeat for 10 minutes; a stale device is not away, decision 435), the device that owns a thread turns the needs-decision reports its threads wait on into the owner's questions itself, under the id the coordinator's own fallback derives from the report's event and at the event's time (decision 409): the documents are identical, so whichever side asks first, the other creates nothing (a 409 from a race reads the existing question). The thread keeps the report's event (thread-local `decisionReport`, optional) and checks before the report leaves, so the question exists before the coordinator can receive the report; the sweeps check again, at most once a minute per project, for threads still waiting for the owner on a needs-decision report this device has not asked about (a coordinator device that went away after the report was relayed); a project without such a thread reads nothing. The report still travels to the coordinator's device. The owner answers such a question as a message to the thread, which any device serves, so it works while the coordinator's device is away. Decision 394's suffix now also covers these reports, and reports a former coordinator device asked about before its queue moved: the coordinator reads a needs-decision report as asked when its derived question is in the project's question list it reads anyway (no extra hub read and no envelope field). Known limits: the fallback needs the hub, where the questions and the roster live, so a coordinator on the hub itself while the hub is down has none; a question answered more than 14 days before the coordinator reads the report is outside the list window (decision 272) and loses the suffix; a coordinator device that comes back at the very moment the owner device reads it as away can receive the report before the question exists and ask again.
513. (D281) Device choices in New thread and Override restart. The work view carries `devices` (optional, `project-device-setup-v1` per device: id, name and an optional reason): every roster device that is not revoked, in roster order, with the reason no thread of the project can run there, from placement's own evaluation: `offline` (which covers stale), `not set up for this project` (no path, or a device the project does not allow), else the per-model account gaps such as `Codex needs login; Claude needs login` when no model that runs threads has an eligible account there. The coordinator's device places threads, so it is the device that always counts as online (decision 243); while the coordinator's device is away this device places (moving the coordinator here is then the only way to start a thread), so the away device reads `offline` rather than being exempted. Running limits only queue a thread and fixed fields are the owner's choice, so neither is a reason; with no model that runs threads at all no device is blamed (placement refuses with its own sentence). The view reads the settings and accounts once per request for the coordinator chip and this list. The interface lists the view's devices, disabled with the label `{name}: {reason}`, keeps the phase gate (decision 294), keeps a current choice the view no longer lists (disabled), and offers the roster's available devices for a view without the list (an older device); Override disables every device until its work view arrived, as before. Offline devices are now listed, disabled, instead of hidden.
514. (D282) Move coordinator here checks this device first. After the move rule (decision 501) and before the assignment, the move checks once with the readiness every coordinator turn's preparation uses (a coordinator model with an eligible account on this device, decision 300, then the project's checkout here, decision 396) and refuses with 409 and that reason (`No enabled model can run the coordinator on {device}.`, `No account can run the coordinator model on {device}.` or the checkout sentence). It applies before any assignment too (a move is an explicit request to run the coordinator here; a first message still assigns as decision 241 says); a repeated move whose assignment already names this device is never refused. The work view keeps `canMoveHere` as the move rule and adds `coordinator.moveRefusal` (optional) where the rule allows the move but this device cannot run the coordinator: the plan's reason, else the path sentence from the settings alone (the view never runs Git). The offline notice (page and New thread dialog) keeps Move coordinator here visible but disabled, with the reason on its own line under it, and the project menu shows the item disabled with the reason under it, so phones see why without a tooltip.
515. (D283) No turn on the former device after a move. Reading the assignment again before the launch only narrows the window, because the move commits on the new device, so the hub orders the two. (1) After its assignment checks (decision 503) the coordinator announces the turn by writing its status `running` on the hub, awaited, before the launch and before a turn number is taken; the hub takes a status only from the assigned device, so a refusal means a move was recorded: the coordinator reads the assignment again, hands the batch over and launches nothing; a hub outage postpones the turn (decision 401); another refusal while the assignment still names this device retries after the retry delay. (2) The hub applies the move rule (decision 501) inside the move's own transaction: a device that is not away and whose own status says `running` keeps the coordinator (409 `The coordinator is working on {device}. Try again when it is idle.`). Every move is therefore ordered against every announced turn: a move recorded before the announcement makes it fail, and an announcement before the move makes the move fail while that device is online. A move away from an offline device still succeeds whatever it published (decision 435), and a turn that device already runs is stopped at its handover (decision 503). Costs: one awaited status write per coordinator turn before the launch (the one after it stays); a turn whose launch then fails publishes idle again through the failure path; a crash between the announcement and the launch leaves `running` on the hub until the device restarts (it publishes its state again) or goes offline.
516. (D284) Evidence for the phase 5 gaps. A page capture of a closed native select cannot show the disabled Device options of decision 513, so the PJ3 New thread journey asserts them in all four layouts: `Automatic`, then every roster device that is not revoked in roster order, `Offline fixture: offline` disabled and `Browser member` enabled. The browser fixture has no device that cannot run the coordinator, so the refusal of decision 514 (offline notice, project menu, New thread dialog) was checked by the builder outside the repository against a fixture server with the work view stubbed to carry the reason, in four layouts, and no committed screenshot shows it. Nothing a committed Projects screenshot shows changed, so the screenshots and vision records stay those of the phase 5 matrix run.
517. (D29) Stopping a main thread commits its leftovers, saves its unpublished commits at `refs/jevellan/discard/{threadId}/1`, returns the checkout to main and releases it as discarded; the stop reason keeps the owner's or the coordinator's words and adds `Its unpublished commits were saved at {ref}.` (decision 526). Since Projects phase 8 a stop first requires the checkout on main with no other agent active in it, and a stop retried after the owner worked in the checkout gives it back as it is (decisions 561 and 576).
518. (D44) Known messages of the conversations' Git workspace are translated into thread words at the Projects boundary, so no thread reason says conversation (decision 530).
519. (D45) A busy publication lease for main is tried again three times, 20 seconds apart, then the thread rests `idle` with `Another publication to main is in progress. Send a message to try again.` (decision 527).
520. (D64) Main isolation needs the device's checkout on main according to its latest heartbeat, when one exists; the rule reads the checkout branch the heartbeat already reports.
521. (D65) Main-isolation candidates exclude devices with a main thread that has not ended or a held checkout claim, derived on the hub from claims and thread indexes (decision 525). A busy checkout excludes the device, so such a start goes to another device or is refused, never queued (decision 283).
522. (D285) Mail and reservation hub operations (brief 5.11 and 7.2). Six operations: `mail-send` (write) stores the mail and answers the stored record; `mail-inbox` (read) answers one page of the thread's unread mail, oldest first by the hub's time then id, cut at 100 mails or when the next one would pass 1 MiB of serialized JSON (always at least one), with `more`; `mail-read` (write, 1 to 100 ids) marks received mail read for the thread; `reserve` (write) takes a `reservation-request` (id, project, thread, paths, reason and `minutes` from 1 to 120) and answers the stored reservation, or none with the conflicts; `release` (write) takes the project, the thread and an optional reservation id; `reservations-list` (read) pages the project's active reservations by id. Reading the inbox marks nothing: the reader marks what it received in a second request, so a lost reply repeats mail instead of losing it, and the mail service returns a page even when its mark fails, and the pages already marked when a later page cannot be read. The hub clock stamps a mail's time and a reservation's creation, expiry and release, and the hub binds a reservation's device to the caller; like the relay's acknowledgement (decision 492), `release` takes no time from the caller. A conflict is one entry per other thread's active reservation with overlapping paths, ordered by creation, at most 100: the thread, its title from the hub's index (null without one), only the holder's paths that overlap the request (in PJ6 a request for `src/app.txt` names `['src/']`) and the expiry; a thread's own reservations never conflict. Authority: when the hub has the thread's index, only its owner device sends its mail, reads or marks its inbox, reserves or releases (403, for example `Only the thread owner can send its mail.`); the coordinator's mail comes only from the assigned coordinator device (403); mail to `coordinator` is refused with 400 because it travels as a coordinator event, as are mail from `all` and new mail with readers; marking mail not addressed to the thread is 403 `This mail is not addressed to that thread.`; releasing another device's reservation is 403, and an unknown id or another thread's is 404 `This reservation was not found.`. Idempotency by id: the same mail (compared without revision, time and readers) returns the stored one and other content is 409 `This mail id was already used for a different message.`; the same reservation id with the same project, thread, device, paths and reason returns the stored one whatever its state since, else 409 `This reservation id was already used for other paths.`. A member checks every reply: the stored mail is the one it sent, inbox records are unread, unique and in order, a mark count stays within the ids, a grant is the requested reservation and a refusal names only other threads' overlapping paths.
523. (D286) Mail reach and retention (brief 5.11 and 7.2). Mail reaches a thread addressed by id, or addressed to `all` when the thread is a main thread other than the sender created at or before the mail's hub time, so a later thread never inherits old broadcasts (a few seconds of skew between the creating device's clock and the hub's are accepted). The sender never receives its own mail to all, and `all` never includes the coordinator, which threads mail only with `to: 'coordinator'`. A thread whose index the hub does not have yet reads only mail addressed to it. Retention, the second half of decision 491: each new mail deletes, in its transaction, the project's mail older than 14 days that every recipient (the project's thread indexes it reaches) read or will never read because it ended, so mail to an id the hub has no index for is not kept forever; each new reservation deletes the project's reservations released or expired more than 24 hours ago. A reservation is active while it is not released and its expiry is after the hub's now. Both prunes touch only the writing project.
524. (D287) Mail and reservation tools (brief 7.1, 7.2 and 9.3). One mail service serves the four main-thread tools and the coordinator's `jevellan_mail_send`. Ids derive from the project, the sender, the turn and the input (decision 415), so a call the transport repeats sends one mail and holds one reservation. A thread's mail to `coordinator` is a `mail` coordinator event whose id derives from the mail id, so the coordinator queue deduplicates it, and it has no hub row. Refusals before sending: mail to itself 400 `Send mail to another thread.`; a `to` that is not `coordinator`, `all` or a thread id 400 `Send mail to coordinator, to a thread id or to all.` (the coordinator's reads `Send mail to a thread id or to all.`); an unknown thread or one of another project 404 `This thread was not found.`; a worktree thread 409 `That thread works in its own worktree and reads no mail.`; an ended thread 409 `That thread has ended and reads no mail.`; `all` without another main thread that has not ended 409 `No other thread works on main right now.`. Recipients are looked up in this device's own threads first (their hub index lags a publish), then on the hub. Inbox entries name senders by title (`(unknown thread)` without an index) and the coordinator as `Coordinator`; a conflict whose holder has no index reads `(unknown thread)`. A reservation whose derived id already ended is asked for again under an id derived from the previous one, following that chain at most 20 hops, so reserve, release and reserve again in one turn holds the paths again while a repeated call still finds the same reservation. The coordinator's chat line names a known thread recipient by title (`Sent mail to "{title}": {subject}`, with the thread id on the line) and mail to all as `Sent mail to all: {subject}`. The filter of decision 307 is gone: the coordinator lists `jevellan_mail_send` (no inbox and no reservations), main threads add the four tools and worktree threads have none. A main thread releases all its reservations when it stops or fails, best effort, because they are advisory and expire; worktree threads make no hub call.
525. (D288) Held checkouts and the main placement rule (brief 10; decisions 520 and 521). The main gate of decision 294 opens: Project settings, New thread and the Override restart offer Main on projects whose Git policy is main (a Leave git project keeps its text). A new hub read, `checkouts-held` (collection `checkouts`), answers for every device with a path in the project the first held checkout claim of that device whose stored path equals the project's raw path or whose owner is a thread or conversation index of the project (member paths are never resolved on the hub), else the project's oldest main thread there that has not ended (its claim may not exist yet): one entry per device (device, owner id and title) in device id order, which members check. Placement reads it only when main is possible (the policy allows it and the isolation is not fixed to worktree) and blocks a device for main, in this order, with this device's own main threads that have not ended (fresher than their hub copies), the main starts this device sent to another device (kept until the hub has their index, or for the dispatch hold time), then the hub's list; during a hub outage only this device's knowledge counts, and the claim at preparation still refuses a busy checkout. The placement packet's active threads now carry their reserved paths from the hub, best effort.
526. (D289) Main thread lifecycle (brief 8.2 step 4, 8.4 and 8.6). A main thread holds the checkout through the daemon's one checkout ownership with the owner `{ conversationId: threadId, conversationTitle: title, workId: threadId }`. Preparation (main runs no setup command): the outside-activity check, the claim, then the conversations' workspace preparation without a branch-change callback; a failure after the claim releases it unchanged, and the thread fails with the refusal as its reason while the coordinator receives the usual interrupted event, because the thread has ended. Every turn and every publication first checks the claim and takes it again when its process is gone after a restart; preparation runs again only while no base commit was stored (decision 280), never after the agent's commits. A thread's own commits are those after its base and not on `origin/main` as last fetched, so an agent that rebased onto newer main itself does not count upstream commits as its own. A stop (decision 517), once the process is gone and for threads past `queued`: without a claim of its own nothing changes; otherwise it takes the claim again, aborts an unfinished rebase, commits leftovers, fetches best effort (offline, the last fetch decides), saves the commits after `merge-base HEAD origin/main` at `refs/jevellan/discard/{threadId}/1` (a ref a crashed stop left at HEAD is reused, any other value refuses), resets the checkout to that base and releases it as discarded; without own commits it releases it unchanged. The stop reason keeps the owner's or the coordinator's words and appends ` Its unpublished commits were saved at {ref}.`. The stop dialog and the restart help of a main thread say so.
527. (D290) Main publication (brief 8.4 and 9.6; decision 519). Publication asserts the claim; refuses with `The checkout is in the middle of a rebase or merge. Finish or abort it, then report done again.` while a rebase, merge, cherry-pick or revert is unfinished, so nothing is committed mid-operation; answers a missing origin as an error; commits leftovers as for worktrees; concludes `no-changes` without own commits; then verifies on a clean checkout: a test run that leaves changes fails with `The test command left changes in the checkout, so this run does not count.`, because the rebase and the push need a clean checkout. Under the checkout's publication lease it makes up to three attempts of fetch, rebase onto `origin/main` when HEAD lacks it (memory-note conflicts merge as in conversations), on a conflict reading the conflicted files and aborting the rebase, after a clean rebase verifying again, then push; three rejected pushes end with `Main changed during publication three times. Send a message to try again.`.
